People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago, I think.\n\nI kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period and I should be able to handle it, but the sleep problems kind of started then and never really went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s normal stuff and everyone worries about these things, but my mind kind of jumps around a lot.\n\nIt’ll go from, like, am I falling behind at work, to am I wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And then even something small can start feeling like it’s going to mess up everything long-term. I know that probably sounds a little exaggerated, but that’s how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things, exactly. I mean, not in a way that sounds obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’ll put off making a call or responding to something if I’m worried it’ll turn into a bigger issue, but I don’t know if that counts. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes.   },

Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago, I think.\n\nI kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period and I should be able to handle it, but the sleep problems kind of started then and never really went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s normal stuff and everyone worries about these things, but my mind kind of jumps around a lot.\n\nIt’ll go from, like, am I falling behind at work, to am I wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And then even something small can start feeling like it’s going to mess up everything long-term. I know that probably sounds a little exaggerated, but that’s how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things, exactly. I mean, not in a way that sounds obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’ll put off making a call or responding to something if I’m worried it’ll turn into a bigger issue, but I don’t know if that counts. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes. By midafternoon at work I feel really drained, like even answering emails takes more effort than it should. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago, I think.\n\nI kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period and I should be able to handle it, but the sleep problems kind of started then and never really went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s normal stuff and everyone worries about these things, but my mind kind of jumps around a lot.\n\nIt’ll go from, like, am I falling behind at work, to am I wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And then even something small can start feeling like it’s going to mess up everything long-term. I know that probably sounds a little exaggerated, but that’s how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things, exactly. I mean, not in a way that sounds obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’ll put off making a call or responding to something if I’m worried it’ll turn into a bigger issue, but I don’t know if that counts. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes. By midafternoon at work I feel really drained, like even answering emails takes more effort than it should. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. I wish it were just one specific thing, because then maybe I could deal with it more logically.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. I wish it were just one specific thing, because then maybe I could deal with it more logically.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but my mind just keeps jumping around—like whether I’m falling behind at work, or wasting money on school, or whether my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd then even small things start feeling like they could somehow mess up my whole future. Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart or anything.

Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying that, because it makes me feel like I’m being a little ridiculous, but it really does feel hard to stop once it starts.

It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but my mind just keeps jumping around—like whether I’m falling behind at work, or wasting money on school, or whether my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd then even small things start feeling like they could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that probably sounds excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. By midafternoon at work I feel really drained, like even answering emails takes more effort than it should. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. I wish it were just one specific thing, because then maybe I could deal with it more logically.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but my mind just keeps jumping around—like whether I’m falling behind at work, or wasting money on school, or whether my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd then even small things start feeling like they could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that probably sounds excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started missing work deadlines, which really bothers me because that’s not usually me. And I’m falling behind in my certificate classes too. Even the normal routines that used to help me stay steady—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of let those go. I keep telling myself I’ll catch up or get back into it, but then I don’t.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying that, because it makes me feel like I’m being a little ridiculous, but it really does feel hard to stop once it starts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but then one worry turns into another. And before I know it I’m checking my calendar, then my budget, then my school assignments over and over… but not actually getting much done.\n\nSo I guess it’s not that I never try to control it. I do try. It just doesn’t really listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being judged or evaluated by others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I care what people think, probably more than I should sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s specifically social situations or being judged in that way.\n\nI guess I worry about how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of that as the main thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago, I think.\n\nI kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period and I should be able to handle it, but the sleep problems kind of started then and never really went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s normal stuff and everyone worries about these things, but my mind kind of jumps around a lot.\n\nIt’ll go from, like, am I falling behind at work, to am I wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And then even something small can start feeling like it’s going to mess up everything long-term. I know that probably sounds a little exaggerated, but that’s how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things, exactly. I mean, not in a way that sounds obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’ll put off making a call or responding to something if I’m worried it’ll turn into a bigger issue, but I don’t know if that counts. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes. By midafternoon at work I feel really drained, like even answering emails takes more effort than it should. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. It’s more just… hard to fully settle, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. I wish it were just one specific thing, because then maybe I could deal with it more logically.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but my mind just keeps jumping around—like whether I’m falling behind at work, or wasting money on school, or whether my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd then even small things start feeling like they could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that probably sounds excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started missing work deadlines, which really bothers me because that’s not usually me. And I’m falling behind in my certificate classes too. Even the normal routines that used to help me stay steady—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of let those go. I keep telling myself I’ll catch up or get back into it, but then I don’t.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying that, because it makes me feel like I’m being a little ridiculous, but it really does feel hard to stop once it starts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but then one worry turns into another. And before I know it I’m checking my calendar, then my budget, then my school assignments over and over… but not actually getting much done.\n\nSo I guess it’s not that I never try to control it. I do try. It just doesn’t really listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being judged or evaluated by others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I care what people think, probably more than I should sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s specifically social situations or being judged in that way.\n\nI guess I worry about how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of that as the main thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are affecting most areas of your life, including work, school, and personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, though I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad.\n\nWork has definitely been affected—I’ve started missing deadlines, which feels awful to admit. And with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. Even in my personal life, I guess… I’ve stopped doing the things that used to keep me grounded, like yoga or running or cooking. I don’t know if that counts as relationships exactly, but it’s like everything is getting squeezed by the worrying.\n\nSo yes, I think it’s more than just one area. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there any part of your life where you feel like your worrying doesn't affect you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve started missing work deadlines and falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to keep me grounded, like yoga, running, and cooking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and I’m not sure there really is.

I mean, some areas might look more “fine” from the outside, but it still sort of seeps in. Work has been the most obvious, because I’ve started missing deadlines, and school too—I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And then the personal routines I used to rely on, like yoga, running, cooking… I’ve kind of let those go.

So I guess I can’t really point to a part of my life where it doesn’t affect me at all. I wish I could. Does that answer what you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess I’m here because my sleep has gotten pretty bad. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s like I’ll be in bed for hours in my apartment, laptop closed, and I’m still going over work deadlines or tuition payments or class assignments in my head.\n\nAnd even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’m not sure if that’s the right thing to come in for, but it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you think might have started these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was one single big event, exactly. It was more… around when I started my evening graduate certificate program, while still working full-time as an analyst. That was about a year and three months ago, I think.\n\nI kept telling myself it was just an adjustment period and I should be able to handle it, but the sleep problems kind of started then and never really went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, not just work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s normal stuff and everyone worries about these things, but my mind kind of jumps around a lot.\n\nIt’ll go from, like, am I falling behind at work, to am I wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And then even something small can start feeling like it’s going to mess up everything long-term. I know that probably sounds a little exaggerated, but that’s how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things, exactly. By midafternoon at work I feel really drained, like even answering emails takes more effort than it should. Sometimes just getting myself to the subway feels like a lot.\n\nAnd I keep thinking if I just rest over the weekend I’ll catch up, but it doesn’t really feel like my energy comes back all the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sad, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or discouraged, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure I’d call it being sad or down most of the time. Is that a helpful distinction?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I’m not sure how much is normal. I do catch myself feeling kind of keyed up sometimes, like I should be doing something or fixing something, even if there isn’t an obvious thing right then.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m pacing around all day or anything. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself avoiding social situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I sometimes hesitate before plans if I’m already feeling wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people because of worry, exactly.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of situation, though. Do you mean like canceling plans or not going to work events because I’m nervous?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I get, like, dramatic trembling or anything that I’ve really noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m worried I feel uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if I’d specifically call it a racing heart or sweating. Maybe I haven’t been paying close enough attention. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes and include symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a sudden wave that peaks in a few minutes. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nI get worried and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’ve had those intense episodes with shortness of breath or my heart racing like you’re describing. I hope that’s the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or anxiety that came on quickly?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve definitely felt anxious, but I don’t think I’ve had that sudden, intense fear that comes on really fast.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like worry than a sudden episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain places or activities because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid places, exactly. I mean, I probably talk myself out of some things if I know I’m going to feel anxious, but I don’t know if that counts as avoidance.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific place I absolutely can’t go to. I just sometimes… choose the easier option, I guess. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry leads you to avoid doing things you would normally want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been trying not to think of it that way, because I don’t want to sound like I’m making excuses, but it has started affecting things.\n\nI’ve missed some work deadlines, which is really not like me, and I’m falling behind in my certificate classes. And even the things that used to help me feel grounded—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of stopped doing them. I tell myself I’ll get back to it, but then I just… don’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you worry about most of the time, or does it feel like your worries are spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of… across a lot of things, honestly. I wish it were just one specific thing, because then maybe I could deal with it more logically.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family is going to think I can’t handle things. And I know some of those probably sound like normal worries, but I treat every little issue like it could somehow derail my whole future.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you mean, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but my mind just keeps jumping around—like whether I’m falling behind at work, or wasting money on school, or whether my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nAnd then even small things start feeling like they could somehow mess up my whole future. I know that probably sounds excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started missing work deadlines, which really bothers me because that’s not usually me. And I’m falling behind in my certificate classes too. Even the normal routines that used to help me stay steady—like yoga, running, cooking—I’ve kind of let those go. I keep telling myself I’ll catch up or get back into it, but then I don’t.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying that, because it makes me feel like I’m being a little ridiculous, but it really does feel hard to stop once it starts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, just focus on one thing,” but then one worry turns into another. And before I know it I’m checking my calendar, then my budget, then my school assignments over and over… but not actually getting much done.\n\nSo I guess it’s not that I never try to control it. I do try. It just doesn’t really listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being judged or evaluated by others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I care what people think, probably more than I should sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s specifically social situations or being judged in that way.\n\nI guess I worry about how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of that as the main thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are affecting most areas of your life, including work, school, and personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, though I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad.\n\nWork has definitely been affected—I’ve started missing deadlines, which feels awful to admit.
